What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is I’m frightened by how I’ve been eating. It’s not just liking food, you know—my wife used to make these big Sunday dinners, pot roast and all, and everyone would go back for seconds, that was normal. But this is different.\n\nWhat scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just keep reaching for more even while I’m telling myself, “Tom, stop this right now.” Like I’m watching myself do it and I can’t quite get hold of the brakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating or the amount you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t feel in control. That’s the part that frightens me. Once I start, it’s like my hands just keep going back for more, even while I’m sitting there telling myself, “Tom, stop this right now.”\n\nIt’s strange, because I’ve had plenty of big meals in my life—Sunday dinners and holidays and all that—but this isn’t the same. This feels like I can see myself doing it, but I can’t quite make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these eating episodes is over?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… ashamed, mostly. Panicky too, if I get on the scale and it’s even up half a pound. I know my doctor keeps saying I’m too thin—he’s said it more than once—but it doesn’t land right in my head.\n\nIt’s silly, maybe, but I’ll see that number and feel like I’ve let myself go, like I need to fix it right away. My father used to check the gas gauge in the car that way, always fussing over little changes—anyway, that’s how I get with the scale. Half a pound and I feel like I’ve done something terrible.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. Even though my doctor keeps telling me I’m too thin, I don’t really feel it that way.\n\nIf the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this panicky feeling, like I’ve done something wrong or let myself go. It’s ridiculous, I know—my wife used to say I could worry a penny smooth if I kept rubbing it—but that number just gets in my head, and then I feel ashamed and like I have to fix it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for what you've eaten by doing something to counteract the calories?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Not throwing up or anything like that, but if I’ve had one of those bad spells, the next day I’ll tell myself, well, no breakfast, no lunch, that’ll even things out.\n\nAnd then I’ll start walking laps, or I’ll get out in the yard and keep doing chores way past when I ought to stop. My knees will be aching and my back will be hollering at me—old house has always got something that needs tending, you know, leaves in the gutter or weeds coming up—but if I stop, I feel like I haven’t paid it back enough. So I just keep going longer than I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, but there’ve been times after I ate too much where I went to the bathroom and made myself bring it back up.\n\nAnd laxatives, too. Not all the time—well, I don’t know, more than I want to admit maybe. I’d get scared the food was just going to sit there in me, like… like when the sink drain backs up and you keep thinking if you just do something, it’ll clear. Sorry, that’s a strange way to put it. But yes, I’ve taken laxatives after, to try to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating where you feel like you can't stop happen?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been happening a lot. I started writing it down because my memory isn’t what it used to be, and the food log makes it harder to pretend.\n\nLooking back at it, nearly every week for many months now there’s been at least one afternoon where I just sort of lose hold of it and eat like I can’t stop. Afternoons are the worst for me—always have been, even back when I’d sit with the newspaper and say I was just having a little something.\n\nAnd then afterward I try to make up for it somehow… throwing up, or taking laxatives, or not eating the next day, or walking and doing chores until I’m worn out. So… yes, almost every week, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is it just what the doctor says?\n\nPatient: I know what he says, and I suppose he’s looking at me with doctor’s eyes, you know… numbers and charts and all that. But I don’t feel that way when I’m standing on the scale.\n\nIf it’s up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky feeling, like I’ve done something wrong. Like I’ve let myself go and now I have to fix it right away. It’s silly, maybe, especially at my age—I mean, half a pound could be anything, could be water, could be my old scale acting up, that thing’s been in the bathroom forever—but it still makes me feel ashamed. So no… I don’t really see “too thin” the way the doctor does.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the way you want it to be?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or I suppose it’s hard to say what I “want,” exactly. I look in the mirror and then I get distracted by the scale, that old bathroom scale with the little crack near the corner—my wife used to tell me it was probably off anyway, you know, but I still check it.\n\nEven when my doctor says I’m too thin, if the number goes up even half a pound, I feel this panic come over me. Like I’ve let myself go somehow, like I’ve been careless and need to fix it right away. And then I feel ashamed, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud at my age, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your daily life or the things you do?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it does. More than I like to admit.\n\nI’ve stopped going to the garden group as much, and I used to like that. We’d talk tomatoes and weeds and whose knees were worse that week… nothing important, but it got me out. Now I think too much about whether there’ll be food there, or whether I’ll be strange about it.\n\nAnd family meals are hard too. I make excuses sometimes. I say I’m tired or not hungry, but really I’m planning around the food before I even get there, or I’m ashamed afterward, or I’m worried someone will notice what I’m doing. My granddaughter is very observant—always has been, even as a little thing—so I get nervous.\n\nSo… yes. It’s gotten into my days. More than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is abnormal, even when others like your doctor tell you it's not?\n\nPatient: I suppose… abnormal is a strong word, isn’t it? My doctor says I’m too thin, and I hear him, I do. He’s a good man, very patient with me—reminds me a bit of my old neighbor who used to explain car engines whether you asked him or not.\n\nBut when I’m at home and I step on that scale, if it’s up even half a pound, I don’t think, “Oh, that’s normal.” I get panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go somehow and I need to fix it right away.\n\nSo no, I don’t see it the way other people do. Even if they tell me I’m not… or that I’m too thin, my head goes straight to the number.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific weight or body shape that you are trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s one exact shape I’m aiming for… not like when my grandson used to cut pictures out of magazines for school projects—though I suppose people do that now on their phones, don’t they?\n\nFor me it’s more the number. Even though my doctor keeps saying I’m too thin, if that scale goes up even half a pound, I feel this awful panic. Ashamed, too. Like I’ve let myself go somehow and I have to fix it right away.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I could name a “goal,” but I know I’m frightened of it going up.\n\nDoctor: How often do you use laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not every time, but there are times after I’ve eaten too much when I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up.\n\nAnd laxatives too… I’m embarrassed saying that. I started keeping them in the cabinet with the old cough drops and bandages—funny how a medicine cabinet becomes a little museum after a while—but yes, I’ve taken them after eating because I get scared the food will just… stay in me. Like I need to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: It happens… well, there are times after I’ve eaten too much that I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t like saying it plain like that.\n\nIt isn’t something I’m proud of. I get scared the food will just stay in me, and then my mind starts racing. Sometimes I’ve taken laxatives too, for the same reason. It’s like I feel I have to undo what happened, even though I know that probably sounds foolish.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve said it, but… yes, after I eat too much, there are times I go into the bathroom and make myself throw up. It’s not every single bite or anything like that, but it happens enough that I can’t pretend it doesn’t.\n\nI get this scared feeling that the food is just sitting there in me, and I have to get it out. Same sort of thinking is why I’ve taken laxatives too sometimes. It’s like I’m trying to undo what I just did.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say you have one of these episodes where you eat and feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to put a number on it… I lose track, you know? My wife used to keep little calendars for appointments, with the church suppers and the dentist written in, and I was never good at keeping count of things like that.\n\nBut what scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just keep reaching for more, even while I’m telling myself, “Tom, stop this right now.” I’ll be thinking it plain as day, and still I keep going. So… it happens enough in a month that it worries me, but I don’t have an exact number.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: It’s… after those times when I’ve eaten too much, that’s when it happens. I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. Not something I like admitting.\n\nI don’t know why my mind gets stuck on it, but I get scared the food is just going to stay in me. My wife used to say I worried things to death, even little things like whether the stove was off, and I suppose this is that same kind of panic, only worse. And sometimes I’ve taken laxatives too, trying to get rid of it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I can’t give you a clean number, Doctor. Some weeks it might be a few times, after I’ve eaten too much and get that panicky feeling. I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself bring it back up.\n\nIt’s not something I plan like, you know, Tuesdays and Thursdays or anything. My wife used to say I never kept track of things unless she wrote them down for me. But it happens enough in a week that I know it’s not just once in a blue moon. And I’ve taken laxatives too sometimes, because I get scared the food is going to stay in me.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do you have these episodes where you eat and feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: Well… I did start writing it down, like you told me, or maybe like my daughter suggested—I can’t remember now. Those little food logs, they’re not pretty to look at. Nearly every week there’s at least one afternoon where I got into that state, where I just kept eating and felt like I couldn’t get the brakes on.\n\nAfternoons are the worst, for some reason. I’ll be puttering around, thinking I’ll just have a bite, and then… well, there it is in the notebook again. And afterward I try to make up for it somehow—throwing up, or taking something, or not eating later, or walking too much, doing chores till I’m worn out.\n\nSo I guess, on average, at least once a week. Most weeks, anyway, for months now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F50.2", "F50.8", "F50.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… it’s there in those logs. I don’t like looking at them, honestly. For many months now, nearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where I ate like I couldn’t stop, and then afterward I tried to undo it somehow.

Sometimes that means making myself throw up, sometimes laxatives. Other times I don’t eat later, or I push myself with walking and chores until I’m worn out. Afternoons seem to be when it happens most, which is funny because I used to always keep busy then—yard work, little repairs, things like that—but now it can turn into this whole mess.

So… nearly every week, at least once, if I’m being honest.

It’s not just liking food, you know—my wife used to make these big Sunday dinners, pot roast and all, and everyone would go back for seconds, that was normal. But this is different.\n\nWhat scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just keep reaching for more even while I’m telling myself, “Tom, stop this right now.” Like I’m watching myself do it and I can’t quite get hold of the brakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating or the amount you're eating when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t feel in control. That’s the part that frightens me. Once I start, it’s like my hands just keep going back for more, even while I’m sitting there telling myself, “Tom, stop this right now.”\n\nIt’s strange, because I’ve had plenty of big meals in my life—Sunday dinners and holidays and all that—but this isn’t the same. This feels like I can see myself doing it, but I can’t quite make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these eating episodes is over?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… ashamed, mostly. Panicky too, if I get on the scale and it’s even up half a pound. I know my doctor keeps saying I’m too thin—he’s said it more than once—but it doesn’t land right in my head.\n\nIt’s silly, maybe, but I’ll see that number and feel like I’ve let myself go, like I need to fix it right away. My father used to check the gas gauge in the car that way, always fussing over little changes—anyway, that’s how I get with the scale. Half a pound and I feel like I’ve done something terrible.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… very much. Even though my doctor keeps telling me I’m too thin, I don’t really feel it that way.\n\nIf the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this panicky feeling, like I’ve done something wrong or let myself go. It’s ridiculous, I know—my wife used to say I could worry a penny smooth if I kept rubbing it—but that number just gets in my head, and then I feel ashamed and like I have to fix it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for what you've eaten by doing something to counteract the calories?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Not throwing up or anything like that, but if I’ve had one of those bad spells, the next day I’ll tell myself, well, no breakfast, no lunch, that’ll even things out.\n\nAnd then I’ll start walking laps, or I’ll get out in the yard and keep doing chores way past when I ought to stop. My knees will be aching and my back will be hollering at me—old house has always got something that needs tending, you know, leaves in the gutter or weeds coming up—but if I stop, I feel like I haven’t paid it back enough. So I just keep going longer than I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, but there’ve been times after I ate too much where I went to the bathroom and made myself bring it back up.\n\nAnd laxatives, too. Not all the time—well, I don’t know, more than I want to admit maybe. I started writing it down because my memory isn’t what it used to be, and the food log makes it harder to pretend.\n\nLooking back at it, nearly every week for many months now there’s been at least one afternoon where I just sort of lose hold of it and eat like I can’t stop. Afternoons are the worst for me—always have been, even back when I’d sit with the newspaper and say I was just having a little something.\n\nAnd then afterward I try to make up for it somehow… throwing up, or taking laxatives, or not eating the next day, or walking and doing chores until I’m worn out. So… yes, almost every week, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is it just what the doctor says?\n\nPatient: I know what he says, and I suppose he’s looking at me with doctor’s eyes, you know… numbers and charts and all that. But I don’t feel that way when I’m standing on the scale.\n\nIf it’s up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky feeling, like I’ve done something wrong. Like I’ve let myself go and now I have to fix it right away. It’s silly, maybe, especially at my age—I mean, half a pound could be anything, could be water, could be my old scale acting up, that thing’s been in the bathroom forever—but it still makes me feel ashamed. So no… I don’t really see “too thin” the way the doctor does.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the way you want it to be?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or I suppose it’s hard to say what I “want,” exactly. I look in the mirror and then I get distracted by the scale, that old bathroom scale with the little crack near the corner—my wife used to tell me it was probably off anyway, you know, but I still check it.\n\nEven when my doctor says I’m too thin, if the number goes up even half a pound, I feel this panic come over me. Like I’ve let myself go somehow, like I’ve been careless and need to fix it right away. And then I feel ashamed, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud at my age, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your daily life or the things you do?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it does. More than I like to admit.\n\nI’ve stopped going to the garden group as much, and I used to like that. We’d talk tomatoes and weeds and whose knees were worse that week… nothing important, but it got me out. Now I think too much about whether there’ll be food there, or whether I’ll be strange about it.\n\nAnd family meals are hard too. I make excuses sometimes. I say I’m tired or not hungry, but really I’m planning around the food before I even get there, or I’m ashamed afterward, or I’m worried someone will notice what I’m doing. My granddaughter is very observant—always has been, even as a little thing—so I get nervous.\n\nSo… yes. It’s gotten into my days. More than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is abnormal, even when others like your doctor tell you it's not?\n\nPatient: I suppose… abnormal is a strong word, isn’t it? My doctor says I’m too thin, and I hear him, I do. He’s a good man, very patient with me—reminds me a bit of my old neighbor who used to explain car engines whether you asked him or not.\n\nBut when I’m at home and I step on that scale, if it’s up even half a pound, I don’t think, “Oh, that’s normal.” I get panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go somehow and I need to fix it right away.\n\nSo no, I don’t see it the way other people do. Even if they tell me I’m not… or that I’m too thin, my head goes straight to the number.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific weight or body shape that you are trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s one exact shape I’m aiming for… not like when my grandson used to cut pictures out of magazines for school projects—though I suppose people do that now on their phones, don’t they?\n\nFor me it’s more the number. Even though my doctor keeps saying I’m too thin, if that scale goes up even half a pound, I feel this awful panic. Ashamed, too. Like I’ve let myself go somehow and I have to fix it right away.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I could name a “goal,” but I know I’m frightened of it going up.\n\nDoctor: How often do you use laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not every time, but there are times after I’ve eaten too much when I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up.\n\nAnd laxatives too… I’m embarrassed saying that. I started keeping them in the cabinet with the old cough drops and bandages—funny how a medicine cabinet becomes a little museum after a while—but yes, I’ve taken them after eating because I get scared the food will just… stay in me. Like I need to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: It happens… well, there are times after I’ve eaten too much that I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t like saying it plain like that.\n\nIt isn’t something I’m proud of. I get scared the food will just stay in me, and then my mind starts racing. Sometimes I’ve taken laxatives too, for the same reason. It’s like I feel I have to undo what happened, even though I know that probably sounds foolish.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve said it, but… yes, after I eat too much, there are times I go into the bathroom and make myself throw up. It’s not every single bite or anything like that, but it happens enough that I can’t pretend it doesn’t.\n\nI get this scared feeling that the food is just sitting there in me, and I have to get it out. It’s like I’m trying to undo what I just did.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say you have one of these episodes where you eat and feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to put a number on it… I lose track, you know? Not throwing up or anything like that, but if I’ve had one of those bad spells, the next day I’ll tell myself, well, no breakfast, no lunch, that’ll even things out.\n\nAnd then I’ll start walking laps, or I’ll get out in the yard and keep doing chores way past when I ought to stop. My knees will be aching and my back will be hollering at me—old house has always got something that needs tending, you know, leaves in the gutter or weeds coming up—but if I stop, I feel like I haven’t paid it back enough. So I just keep going longer than I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. I don’t like saying that, but there’ve been times after I ate too much where I went to the bathroom and made myself bring it back up.\n\nAnd laxatives, too. Not all the time—well, I don’t know, more than I want to admit maybe. I’d get scared the food was just going to sit there in me, like… like when the sink drain backs up and you keep thinking if you just do something, it’ll clear. Sorry, that’s a strange way to put it. But yes, I’ve taken laxatives after, to try to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating where you feel like you can't stop happen?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been happening a lot. I started writing it down because my memory isn’t what it used to be, and the food log makes it harder to pretend.\n\nLooking back at it, nearly every week for many months now there’s been at least one afternoon where I just sort of lose hold of it and eat like I can’t stop. Afternoons are the worst for me—always have been, even back when I’d sit with the newspaper and say I was just having a little something.\n\nAnd then afterward I try to make up for it somehow… throwing up, or taking laxatives, or not eating the next day, or walking and doing chores until I’m worn out. So… yes, almost every week, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is it just what the doctor says?\n\nPatient: I know what he says, and I suppose he’s looking at me with doctor’s eyes, you know… numbers and charts and all that. But I don’t feel that way when I’m standing on the scale.\n\nIf it’s up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky feeling, like I’ve done something wrong. Like I’ve let myself go and now I have to fix it right away. It’s silly, maybe, especially at my age—I mean, half a pound could be anything, could be water, could be my old scale acting up, that thing’s been in the bathroom forever—but it still makes me feel ashamed. So no… I don’t really see “too thin” the way the doctor does.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the way you want it to be?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or I suppose it’s hard to say what I “want,” exactly. I look in the mirror and then I get distracted by the scale, that old bathroom scale with the little crack near the corner—my wife used to tell me it was probably off anyway, you know, but I still check it.\n\nEven when my doctor says I’m too thin, if the number goes up even half a pound, I feel this panic come over me. Like I’ve let myself go somehow, like I’ve been careless and need to fix it right away. And then I feel ashamed, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud at my age, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect your daily life or the things you do?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it does. More than I like to admit.\n\nI’ve stopped going to the garden group as much, and I used to like that. We’d talk tomatoes and weeds and whose knees were worse that week… nothing important, but it got me out. Now I think too much about whether there’ll be food there, or whether I’ll be strange about it.\n\nAnd family meals are hard too. I make excuses sometimes. I say I’m tired or not hungry, but really I’m planning around the food before I even get there, or I’m ashamed afterward, or I’m worried someone will notice what I’m doing. My granddaughter is very observant—always has been, even as a little thing—so I get nervous.\n\nSo… yes. It’s gotten into my days. More than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight is abnormal, even when others like your doctor tell you it's not?\n\nPatient: I suppose… abnormal is a strong word, isn’t it? My doctor says I’m too thin, and I hear him, I do. He’s a good man, very patient with me—reminds me a bit of my old neighbor who used to explain car engines whether you asked him or not.\n\nBut when I’m at home and I step on that scale, if it’s up even half a pound, I don’t think, “Oh, that’s normal.” I get panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go somehow and I need to fix it right away.\n\nSo no, I don’t see it the way other people do. Even if they tell me I’m not… or that I’m too thin, my head goes straight to the number.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific weight or body shape that you are trying to achieve?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s one exact shape I’m aiming for… not like when my grandson used to cut pictures out of magazines for school projects—though I suppose people do that now on their phones, don’t they?\n\nFor me it’s more the number. Even though my doctor keeps saying I’m too thin, if that scale goes up even half a pound, I feel this awful panic. Ashamed, too. Like I’ve let myself go somehow and I have to fix it right away.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I could name a “goal,” but I know I’m frightened of it going up.\n\nDoctor: How often do you use laxatives or diuretics to try to counteract the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not every time, but there are times after I’ve eaten too much when I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up.\n\nAnd laxatives too… I’m embarrassed saying that. I started keeping them in the cabinet with the old cough drops and bandages—funny how a medicine cabinet becomes a little museum after a while—but yes, I’ve taken them after eating because I get scared the food will just… stay in me. Like I need to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: It happens… well, there are times after I’ve eaten too much that I go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. I don’t like saying it plain like that.\n\nIt isn’t something I’m proud of. I get scared the food will just stay in me, and then my mind starts racing. Sometimes I’ve taken laxatives too, for the same reason. It’s like I feel I have to undo what happened, even though I know that probably sounds foolish.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve said it, but… yes, after I eat too much, there are times I go into the bathroom and make myself throw up. It’s not every single bite or anything like that, but it happens enough that I can’t pretend it doesn’t.\n\nI get this scared feeling that the food is just sitting there in me, and I have to get it out. Same sort of thinking is why I’ve taken laxatives too sometimes. It’s like I’m trying to undo what I just did.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say you have one of these episodes where you eat and feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to put a number on it… I lose track, you know? My wife used to keep little calendars for appointments, with the church suppers and the dentist written in, and I was never good at keeping count of things like that.\n\nBut what scares me most is that once I start eating, it feels like my hands just keep reaching for more, even while I’m telling myself, “Tom, stop this right now.” I’ll be thinking it plain as day, and still I keep going. So… it happens enough in a month that it worries me, but I don’t have an exact number.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: It’s… after those times when I’ve eaten too much, that’s when it happens. I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. Not something I like admitting.\n\nI don’t know why my mind gets stuck on it, but I get scared the food is just going to stay in me. My wife used to say I worried things to death, even little things like whether the stove was off, and I suppose this is that same kind of panic, only worse. And sometimes I’ve taken laxatives too, trying to get rid of it somehow.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I can’t give you a clean number, Doctor. Some weeks it might be a few times, after I’ve eaten too much and get that panicky feeling. I’ll go into the bathroom and make myself bring it back up.\n\nIt’s not something I plan like, you know, Tuesdays and Thursdays or anything. My wife used to say I never kept track of things unless she wrote them down for me. But it happens enough in a week that I know it’s not just once in a blue moon. And I’ve taken laxatives too sometimes, because I get scared the food is going to stay in me.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do you have these episodes where you eat and feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: Well… I did start writing it down, like you told me, or maybe like my daughter suggested—I can’t remember now. Those little food logs, they’re not pretty to look at. Nearly every week there’s at least one afternoon where I got into that state, where I just kept eating and felt like I couldn’t get the brakes on.\n\nAfternoons are the worst, for some reason. I’ll be puttering around, thinking I’ll just have a bite, and then… well, there it is in the notebook again. And afterward I try to make up for it somehow—throwing up, or taking something, or not eating later, or walking too much, doing chores till I’m worn out.\n\nSo I guess, on average, at least once a week. Most weeks, anyway, for months now.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself throw up or use laxatives after one of these episodes of eating too much?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s there in those logs. I don’t like looking at them, honestly. For many months now, nearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where I ate like I couldn’t stop, and then afterward I tried to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that means making myself throw up, sometimes laxatives. Other times I don’t eat later, or I push myself with walking and chores until I’m worn out. Afternoons seem to be when it happens most, which is funny because I used to always keep busy then—yard work, little repairs, things like that—but now it can turn into this whole mess.\n\nSo… nearly every week, at least once, if I’m being honest."
  }
